# IN-HOME SERVICES POLICY, PROTOCOL, AND GUIDANCE

## Purpose

The primary goal of opening an ongoing case for Child Protective Services In-Home Services is to support families in safely maintaining their child(ren) in their own home by eliminating identified danger indicators and reducing risk of future child maltreatment. This is achieved through engagement of the family and strengthening and building of their Safety Network.

In-Home Services provides interventions and services to families after maltreatment has occurred. CPS In-Home Services are legally mandated and are provided to:

• Address child safety and risk of future maltreatment and protection; • Preserve families (maintain child(ren) safely in their home); and • Prevent further abuse or neglect by strengthening the family's capacity to protect and nurture their children. In-Home Services:

• Provides the most intensive services and contacts to families with the greatest needs, while those with fewer needs receive fewer intensive services/contacts; • Delivers services within the context of the family's own community; • Enables caseworkers to better identify risks in their work with families; • Engages children, youth, and families in the planning process while producing better outcomes of safety, permanence, and well-being for children; and • Encourages families to develop Circles of Safety and Support and support their Safety Network in participating in the planning for coping with future challenges.

The North Carolina Division of Social Services and local county child welfare agencies are prohibited from discriminating for reasons of race, ethnicity, color, national origin, sex, religion, age, disability, and/or political beliefs. This includes when working with youth, families, parents, caregivers, kinship providers, and licensed foster parents.

Note: CPS-A refers to the CPS Assessment.

# Required Timeframes

| Required Timeframes | |
|---|---|
|• Date of case decision|• An ongoing case is opened, and In-Home Services begins|
|• Within 7 days of case decision|• Face-to-face contact with family<br>• Refer to Evidenced Based Prevention Service Policy if a service from NC Title IV-E Evidence-Based<br>Prevention Services Plan is recommended|
|• Within 30 days of case decision|• North Carolina Trauma Screening Tool (DSS-6195a & 6195b-based on child's age) (if not completed<br>during the Assessment)<br>• Review of NC Safety Assessment<br>• Non-Offending Parent/Adult Victim Domestic Violence Assessment Tool (DSS-5235) and the Domestic<br>Violence Perpetrator Assessment Tool (DSS-5234) (if not completed during the Assessment)<br>• If placed with a Temporary Safety Provider during Assessments, the Comprehensive Provider<br>Assessment (& approved by supervisor)<br>• Development ofIn-Home Family Case Planand integration of thePlan of Safe Care (POSC) (DSS-6191), <br>if applicable<br>• Child/Family Team Meeting, if an active Safety Plan is necessary (must include Safety Network<br>members identified in the Safety Plan)|
|• Within 4 months of case decision<br>(90 days after development of initialIn-<br>Home Family Case Plan/CFT) & every<br>90 days thereafter& after the<br>completion of anEvidence-Based<br>Prevention Services|• CFT to review and/or update theIn-Home Family Case Plan. This should include a review of the Safey<br>Assessment completed by the Assessment caseworker OR the Progress to Case Closure Guide<br>completed by IH, whichever is most recent, and the resulting Safety Plan.<br>• Progress to Case Closure Guide<br>• Family Strengths and Needs Assessment<br>• Child Strengths and Needs Assessment, if applicable|
|• Within 6 months of development of<br>theIn-Home Family Case Plan|• Case review regarding family progress and local county child welfare agency determination about<br>status of In-Home Services case<br>• Progress to Case Closure Guide<br>• Family Strengths and Needs Assessment<br>• Child Strengths and Needs Assessment, if applicable|

<!-- page: 9 -->
# Assessing Safety and Risk of Maltreatment

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|Once the CPS-A recommendation is for the case to be transferred, an Ongoing case<br>for In-Home Services is opened. During In-Home Services the local county child<br>welfare agency must monitor and assess child(ren)'s safety and risk which includes,<br>but is not limited to:<br>•<br>Ongoing contacts that:<br>`o` Include an interview with every child<br>`o` Be alone for at least part of the visit,<br>`o` With all family members that live in the home; and<br>`o` Contact collaterals at the frequency required;<br>•<br>Engagement with each family member that includes an assessment of the<br>strengths and needs. An effective In-Homecaseworker willproperly assess<br>andapply methods and skills from motivational interviewing and<br>strengths based, solution focused interventionsto support<br>change. <br>CHRONIC NEGLECT/ RE-OCCURRENCE OF MALTREATMENT<br>Although families are receiving services, there are times when<br>families continue to have incidents of maltreatment. This cycle<br>may happen several times leading to a description of the<br>incidents or choices the family makes as being chronic, or a<br>frequent occurrence of maltreatment.<br>One of the underlying beliefs of the family-centered approach<br>continues to be that the safety of the child is the first concern.<br>Family members should be encouraged to address and share<br>concerns regarding the recurring neglect. TheIn-Home<br>caseworker should explain that the primary goal is to maintain<br>the child safely in the home of the caretaker, but if the child's<br>safety is compromised the local county child welfare agency will<br>take steps to ensure the safety of the child. # Assessing Safety and Risk of Maltreatment

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|FAMILY STRENGTHS AND NEEDS ASSESSMENT (FSNA)<br>The following is an abbreviated overview of the SDM Family Strengths and Needs<br>Assessment (FSNA). The SDM FSNA Manual must be reviewed for comprehensive<br>policies, procedures, and completion instructions.<br>**Purpose:**<br>From assessments to in-home to permanency planning, the Family Strengths and<br>Needs Assessment (FSNA) is a Structured Decision Making (SDM) tool that is<br>consistently updated throughout the life of the case.<br>The FSNA tool (caretaker and child domains) examines key aspects of family and<br>child functioning and guides caseworkers to the central question:_What are the_<br>_most important needs to address in the case plan, and what strengths can we build_<br>_on?_<br>The**top three priority needs** identified through the FSNA become the basis for the<br>objectives/goals in the In-Home Family Case Plan (IHFCP). By explicitly naming a<br>family's strengths and natural supports, the FSNA helps caseworkers, supervisors,<br>and safety network members engage in a** collaborative assessment process** that<br>empowers families and grounds the case plan in their own capacity to create<br>safety. This strengths-focused approach reinforces child safety while guiding<br>effective and meaningful case planning.<br>**Structure:**<br>The FSNA has two core parts:<br>1. Caretaker Strengths and Needs Assessment<br>•<br>All cases where a decision has been made to provide child protective<br>services (CPS) In-Home or services.<br>•<br>Complete on the caretakers of origin in the maltreating household; do not<br>complete on kinship homes, residential treatment providers, or licensed<br>family fosterparents.|FAMILY STRENGTHS AND NEEDS ASSESSMENT (FSNA)<br>The following is an abbreviated overview of the SDM Family Strengths and Needs<br>Assessment (FSNA). Child Strengths and Needs Assessment<br>There are two circumstances when a child is transferring to In-Home services in<br>which the child SNA should be completed:<br>•<br>All cases transferring from a family assessment or investigative assessment<br>to In-Home where the caseworker selected one of the challenging child<br>characteristics on the FSNA. |PROGRESS TO CASE CLOSURE GUIDE (PCCG)<br>The following is an abbreviated overview of the SDM Progress to Case Closure<br>Guide (PCCG). The SDM Progress to Case Closure Guide Manual must be reviewed<br>for comprehensive policies, procedures, and completion instructions.<br>**Purpose:**<br>•<br>The purpose of the progress to case closure guide is to help caseworkers<br>assess whether danger indicators have been resolved and the caretaker has<br>demonstrated sufficient Family Case Plan progress to allow a case to be closed<br>or whether services should continue. This is accomplished through evaluating<br>whether the caretaker's behaviors and actions have changed.<br>•<br>TheProgress to Case Closure Guide (PCCG) combines items from the SDM<br>Safety Assessment with items that evaluate a family's progress toward Family<br>Case Plan goals.<br>In Home caseworkers must review the most recent Safety Assessment (completed<br>in CPS Assessments) before completing the PCCG.<br>**NOTE**: If a new danger indicator arises during an In-Home case (for example, the<br>case was opened for severe neglect and a new allegation of sexual abuse is made),<br>the In-Home caseworker must make a report to Intake. If Intake accepts the<br>report, a new SDM Safety Assessment must be completed that addresses all<br>current and newly identified danger indicators. Any new allegation or incident that<br>meets the statutory definition of abuse, neglect, or dependency during CPS In-<br>Home Services must be entered in PATH-NC as a new report and screened through<br>Intake using the SDM Screening and Response Tool. If the report meets the<br>screening threshold, a CPS Assessment must be initiated. |NORTH CAROLINA TRAUMA SCREENING TOOL<br>All children receiving In-Home services must be screened using the North Carolina<br>Trauma Tool (DSS-6195A &6195B). There are two versions of the screening tool,<br>and the caseworker must use the screening tool that aligns with the child's age, 0-<br>5 or 6-21.<br>For a child(ren) screened during the Assessment phase, the In-Home caseworker<br>must review the completed screening tool and incorporate, as needed, into the In-<br>Home Family Case Plan. In cases where a Trauma Screening Tool was not<br>completed in the Assessment phase, it must be completed during the In-Home<br>case.<br>The child must also be re-screened if one of the following circumstances occur:<br>•<br>When new or additional information is obtained during the provision of In-<br>Home Services that indicates a trauma has occurred<br>•<br>If there is another incident of maltreatment<br>•<br>If the child's behavior deteriorates<br>•<br>If the child has to be removed from the home (i.e. TSP or agency custody)|The North Carolina Trauma Screening Tools have been<br>developed to assist caseworkers in identifying trauma that can<br>impact safety and wellbeing for children. The tool should be<br>used to support In-Home caseworkers in determining whether<br>additional mental health services are needed. It can also be used<br>to inform how In-Home caseworkers interact with the family as<br>well as situations and circumstances that may be triggering for<br>that child. The ability to identify potential triggers can help<br>inform caseworkers practice and reduce future traumas. |QUALITY OF CONTACTS<br>A quality contact is one that is sufficient to ensure the safety, permanency and<br>well-being of the child(ren). It will also help to<br>assess where the family has gaps that can be addressed to ensure<br>safety for the children. Identifying and engaging relatives should<br>be an ongoing process throughout In-Home services.<br>Some families may identify individuals that are not biologically<br>related to them as relatives. For families with limited biological<br>supportsnon-relative kin can be an essential support to those<br>families and should be engaged in the process of assisting families<br>with keeping children safe. For the purposes of engagement in<br>building the Safety Network, anynon-relative kin identified as<br>relatives by the family should be treated as such.<br>N.C.G.S § 7B-101 defines nonrelative kin as an individual having a<br>substantial relationship with the child. This includes any member<br>of a State or federally recognized tribe regardless of their<br>relationship if the child(ren) is a member of a tribe.<br>Safety Networks can help families reach their safety goals. The<br>Circle of Safety and Support can be used to identify a broad range<br>of individuals to engage in support of the family. This may include<br>natural or professional supportswith nocaregiving responsibilities<br>who can beparticipants thatprovide support to the safety plan|In-Homecaseworkers should gather as much information as<br>possible about a family's Safety Network. For families with limited biological<br>supportsnon-relative kin can be an essential support to those<br>families and should be engaged in the process of assisting families<br>with keeping children safe. For the purposes of engagement in<br>building the Safety Network, anynon-relative kin identified as<br>relatives by the family should be treated as such.<br>N.C.G.S § 7B-101 defines nonrelative kin as an individual having a<br>substantial relationship with the child. This includes any member<br>of a State or federally recognized tribe regardless of their<br>relationship if the child(ren) is a member of a tribe.<br>Safety Networks can help families reach their safety goals. The<br>Circle of Safety and Support can be used to identify a broad range<br>of individuals to engage in support of the family. This may include<br>natural or professional supportswith nocaregiving responsibilities<br>who can beparticipants thatprovide support to the safety plan|

# Required Contacts for In Home Services

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|If a direct threat is heard by theIn-Home caseworker, they must take immediate<br>steps to protect themselves, the children and/or non-offending parent/adult<br>victim.|If a direct threat is heard by theIn-Home caseworker, they must take immediate<br>steps to protect themselves, the children and/or non-offending parent/adult<br>victim.|If a direct threat is heard by theIn-Home caseworker, they must take immediate<br>steps to protect themselves, the children and/or non-offending parent/adult<br>victim.||||
|INITIAL FAMILY/CHILD CONTACT<br>Within seven days of the case decision (Substantiation or a finding of Child<br>Protective Services Needed for any report of maltreatment, face-to-face contact<br>with the family by anIn-Home caseworker must occur (the only exception is for<br>cases that are substantiated and closed). This contact:<br>•<br>Begins the transition from the CPS-A to CPS In-Home Services;<br>•<br>Informs the parent(s)/caretaker(s) of the reason and purpose for In-<br>Home Services;<br>•<br>Must include a review of the Continuing Needs and Safety<br>Requirements. This page must be signed by the parent/custodian to<br>ensure the parents understand the concerns and the actions<br>believed to address those concerns and stated requirements to<br>maintain the child(s) safety (including use, if necessary, of a<br>Temporary Safety Provider). If the parent refuses to sign and<br>verbally refuses to agree to its provisions, safety must be ensured<br>regardless of whether the child is in their own home or in another<br>type of arrangement. A copy must be provided to the family.<br>•<br>Must include a review of and any updates to thePlan of Safe Care<br>(DSS-6191), should the familyhave a child that has been identified<br>as asubstance affected infant;<br>•<br>Review and refer toEvidenced Based Prevention Service Policy if a<br>service from NC Title IV-E Evidence-Based Prevention Servicesand<br>•<br>Includes discussion about the development of the IHFCP within 30<br>days of the case decision.|INITIAL FAMILY/CHILD CONTACT<br>Within seven days of the case decision (Substantiation or a finding of Child<br>Protective Services Needed for any report of maltreatment, face-to-face contact<br>with the family by anIn-Home caseworker must occur (the only exception is for<br>cases that are substantiated and closed). |REQUIRED ONGOING CONTACTS<br>Moderate risk cases require minimum contacts for all CPS In-Home Services that<br>includes:<br>Face-to-face contact with victim children:<br>•<br>To meet requirements, the contact must be of quality and sufficient to<br>ensure the safety, permanency and well-being of the child. It must also<br>includecontact with each child separately from the parent/caretaker as<br>least part of the time for all children over 12 months old.<br>•<br>Must be at least 2 times per month: at least one visit in the first half of<br>the month and the second visit in the second half of the month OR at<br>least 15 days apart<br>•<br>Additional visits as needed at intervals to assure the child's safety<br>•<br>Must include observation of the interaction and relationship between<br>the child(ren) and parent/caretaker at least once a month; and<br>•<br>At least one of the contacts must occur in the home if the child is not<br>with a Temporary Safety Provider.<br>Face-to-face contact with parents/primary caretaker:<br>•<br>Must be at least 2 times per month: at least one visit in the first half of<br>the month and the second visit in the second half of the month or at<br>least 15 days apart<br>•<br>Mustinclude review of the progress towards the behavioral change<br>identified on theIHFCP|REQUIRED ONGOING CONTACTS<br>Moderate risk cases require minimum contacts for all CPS In-Home Services that<br>includes:<br>Face-to-face contact with victim children:<br>•<br>To meet requirements, the contact must be of quality and sufficient to<br>ensure the safety, permanency and well-being of the child. It<br>is also important to see the children in the home to observe the<br>conditions, and to gain a perspective about the level of safety and<br>continuing risk. A home visit provides firsthand knowledge of the<br>home environment and facilitates the observation of family<br>interactions in the everyday setting. Assessing the physical home<br>environment should be focused on where the child(ren) sleeps,<br>eats, plays, etc.<br>Observations of the home that include infants under 12 months<br>require a check of the sleeping arrangements.For information<br>regarding sleep related infant deaths and recommendations to<br>reduce the risk of occurrence, please refer tothe Safe Sleep<br>section of the Cross Function Manual.<br>Non-Victim Children<br>Examples of a childwho is not identified to have been maltreated<br>living in the home could be, but are not limited to:<br>•<br>A child born to the family during In-Home services with no<br>finding of substantiation or Child Protective Services<br>Needed and no risk of maltreatment is assessed (A new<br>Intake report is required); <br>•<br>A child that moved into the home after the In-Home<br>Services case opened and after the abuse or neglect<br>occurredand no risk of maltreatment is assessed; and|It is important to build trust with the child and parent/caretaker. |Face-to-face contact with other household members:<br>•<br>Includes childrenwho are not identified as having been maltreatedin<br>the home; and<br>•<br>Must occur at least once a month.<br>Contact with non-resident parents:<br>•<br>When the non-resident parent has not been located, the In-Home<br>caseworker must make attempts to identify or locate a parent monthly,<br>at minimum.<br>•<br>Identified non-resident parents must be contacted who were not<br>responsible or associated with the danger or risk of harm to the child<br>must be seen at least monthly. <!-- page: 27 -->
# Required Contacts for In Home Services

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|•<br>Must occur at least once a week; and<br>•<br>Must include review of the progress towards the behavioral change<br>identified on the IHFCP<br>•<br>If the child(ren) are in a TSP, must include a review of the Safety<br>Assessment and discussion regarding progress to alleviate the<br>immediate danger indicators.<br>Face-to-face contact with other household members:<br>•<br>Includes all other children in the home; and<br>•<br>Must occur at least twice a month.<br>Face to face contact with Temporary Safety Provider:<br>•<br>Must occur at least once a week;<br>•<br>Discussion regarding the progress to alleviate the immediate danger<br>indicators.<br>Documentation must include the diligent efforts made and/or rationale for<br>contacts not completed at the frequency specified above.<br>SeeTable of In-Home Services Required Contacts<br>REDUCTION OF FREQUENCY OF CONTACTS<br>Contact frequencydetermined by the Risk Assessment at the time of case<br>decision must continue untilthe case is staffed for a reduction of contacts with a<br>two-level review and the reason for that reduction is documented. The In-Home<br>caseworker and supervisor must determine case contacts through use of the<br>Contact Staffing Tool found within case contacts in PATH NC. |The option to reduce the number of required monthly contacts, on a case must<br>only occur with the supervisor and theIn-Home caseworker discussing the<br>rationale, and must:<br>•<br>Occur after a discussion with the family and collaterals;<br>•<br>Be based on the family's progress on the identified behaviors;<br>•<br>Include demonstratedlessening ofdanger and riskof harm in the home;<br>and<br>•<br>Be clearly documented.<br>Children in a TSP must be seen at the frequency of a high risk case. |HOME VISITS<br>At least once per month, theIn-Home caseworker must assess the physical<br>home environment for safety and risk_. _However, theIn-Home caseworker must<br>not enter a home without at least one of the following:<br>•<br>The permission of the parent or person responsible (adult) for the<br>juvenile's care;|HOME VISITS<br>At least once per month, theIn-Home caseworker must assess the physical<br>home environment for safety and risk_. _However, theIn-Home caseworker must<br>not enter a home without at least one of the following:<br>•<br>The permission of the parent or person responsible (adult) for the<br>juvenile's care;|HOME VISITS<br>At least once per month, theIn-Home caseworker must assess the physical<br>home environment for safety and risk_. _However, theIn-Home caseworker must<br>not enter a home without at least one of the following:<br>•<br>The permission of the parent or person responsible (adult) for the<br>juvenile's care;||||

|**RESPONDING TO MISSING CHILDREN**<br>REPORTING REQUIREMENTS<br>North Carolina law requires a report to be made to law enforcement when a<br>child is believed to be missing, abducted, or runaway.NCGS § 14-318.5 (c)states,<br>"any person who reasonably suspects the disappearance of a child and who<br>reasonably suspects that the child may be in danger shall report those suspicions<br>to law enforcement within a reasonable time..."<br>REQUIREMENTS FOR IN-HOME CASEWORKERS|**RESPONDING TO MISSING CHILDREN**<br>REPORTING REQUIREMENTS<br>North Carolina law requires a report to be made to law enforcement when a<br>child is believed to be missing, abducted, or runaway.NCGS § 14-318.5 (c)states,<br>"any person who reasonably suspects the disappearance of a child and who<br>reasonably suspects that the child may be in danger shall report those suspicions<br>to law enforcement within a reasonable time..."<br>REQUIREMENTS FOR IN-HOME CASEWORKERS|**RESPONDING TO MISSING CHILDREN**<br>REPORTING REQUIREMENTS<br>North Carolina law requires a report to be made to law enforcement when a<br>child is believed to be missing, abducted, or runaway.NCGS § 14-318.5 (c)states,<br>"any person who reasonably suspects the disappearance of a child and who<br>reasonably suspects that the child may be in danger shall report those suspicions<br>to law enforcement within a reasonable time..."<br>REQUIREMENTS FOR IN-HOME CASEWORKERS|**RESPONDING TO MISSING CHILDREN**<br>The In-Home caseworker should work to obtain as much<br>information about the child as they can about the circumstances<br>around the child's disappearance. Children who are missing,<br>abducted, or have runaway should be considered at a higher risk<br>for Human Trafficking. For older youth who have been reported as<br>runaways, it will be critical that the In-Home caseworker assess<br>the reasons the child fled as a part of the safety concerns that are<br>evaluated in the home. These may need to be addressed and<br>included in the IHFCP.|**RESPONDING TO MISSING CHILDREN**<br>The In-Home caseworker should work to obtain as much<br>information about the child as they can about the circumstances<br>around the child's disappearance. Children who are missing,<br>abducted, or have runaway should be considered at a higher risk<br>for Human Trafficking. For older youth who have been reported as<br>runaways, it will be critical that the In-Home caseworker assess<br>the reasons the child fled as a part of the safety concerns that are<br>evaluated in the home. These may need to be addressed and<br>included in the IHFCP.|**RESPONDING TO MISSING CHILDREN**<br>The In-Home caseworker should work to obtain as much<br>information about the child as they can about the circumstances<br>around the child's disappearance. Children who are missing,<br>abducted, or have runaway should be considered at a higher risk<br>for Human Trafficking. For older youth who have been reported as<br>runaways, it will be critical that the In-Home caseworker assess<br>the reasons the child fled as a part of the safety concerns that are<br>evaluated in the home. These may need to be addressed and<br>included in the IHFCP.|

# Required Contacts for In Home Services

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|When a child that the local county child welfare agency is actively involved with<br>is missing, abducted, or runaway, the In-Home caseworker must:<br>•<br>Engage the parent/legal caretaker in making a report to law<br>enforcement as soon as it is known that the child/youth is missing,<br>abducted, or runaway;**OR**<br>•<br>Make a report to the local law enforcement, if the parent/legal<br>caretaker refuses.<br>NOTE: A copy of the law enforcement report or the report number must be<br>obtained for the case file.<br>If a parent/legal caretaker refuses to report their child/youth missing, the In-<br>Home caseworker mustengage, assess and document the safety and risk<br>factors related to the missing, abducted, or runaway child/youth and continue<br>to address those concerns throughout the life of the case in addition to any<br>other maltreatment, safety or risk concerns.<br>•<br>Document contacts and the continuing efforts to collaborate with law<br>enforcement, parent/legal caretaker, family members, collaterals, and<br>other appropriate persons to locate the child/youth.<br>•<br>The In-Home caseworker must continue to assist in locating the missing<br>child/youth and make every effort to return the child to an authorized<br>placement throughout the life of the case.<br>•<br>Update any newly identified needs on the IHFCP.<br>If a missing child/youth has not been located at the time of case closure the case<br>must be staffed for a third level of review with a program<br>manager/administrator or higher, and the Regional Child Welfare Specialist must<br>be notified. This must be documented in the case record. This must be documented in the case record. This must be documented in the case record. At case closure, law<br>enforcement must be notified of the decision of the local child welfare agency.|(Additional resources in the Human Trafficking section of the Child<br>WelfareCross FunctionManual).<br>Abduction of Children **_- _**Per N.C.G.S. § 14-41 "any person who,<br>without legal justification or defense, abducts or induces any<br>minor child who is at least four years younger than the person, to<br>leave any person, agency, or institution lawfully entitled to the<br>child's custody, placement, or care..."<br>Kidnapping**_ -_** Per N.C.G.S. § 14-39"any person who shall<br>unlawfully confine, restrain, or remove from one place to another,<br>any other person 16 years of age or over without the consent of<br>such person, or any other person under the age of 16 years<br>without the consent of a parent or legal custodian of such<br>person..."<br>Missing Child **_-_** PerN.C.G.S. § 143B-1011, "a juvenile as defined in<br>N.C.G.S § 7B-101whose location has not been determined, who<br>has been reported as missing to a law enforcement agency, and<br>whose parent's, spouse's, guardian's, or legal custodian's<br>temporary or permanent residence is in North Carolina or is<br>believed to be in North Carolina."<br>When a parent is refusing to report the missing, abducted, or<br>runaway child or youth to law enforcement, the In-Home<br>caseworker should consider whether this constitutes the neglect<br>of the child.NCGS § 14-318.5 (b)states, "A parent or any other<br>person providing care to or supervision of a child who knowingly<br>or wantonly fails to report the disappearance of a child to law<br>enforcement is in violation... is punishable as a Class I felony." ||||that could severely compromise the safety of a child or youth who<br>is missing to include a parent's refusal to report their child missing.<br>When the local county child welfare agency has the responsibility<br>for care and placement of a child, there is the requirement to<br>make notification to the National Center for Missing and Exploited<br>Children (NCMEC) in addition to law enforcement. For foster care<br>cases with an open Assessment, the assigned Permanency<br>caseworker is best suited to provide this notification because they<br>will have the most information about the child's appearance, last<br>known location, and whereabouts. Please see Permanency<br>Planningfor requirements to report.<br>Contacts with Missing Children and Youth<br>If an In-Home caseworker is able to make contact with the missing<br>child/youth, helpful information to attempt to gather from the<br>child or youth would be:<br>•<br>The child's location at the time of contact;<br>•<br>Any information about where they are staying currently or for<br>any period;<br>•<br>Any information about the individuals they may be with;<br>•<br>Information about the health and safety of the child/youth (if<br>parenting, also inquire about the health and safety of the<br>infant or child);<br>•<br>Whether they are attending school and where;<br>•<br>Whether they are employed and where; and<br>•<br>Any contact they have made with family members, friends,<br>probation and parole agents, etc.<br>If possible, consider referencing the Child Welfare Human<br>Trafficking Screening Tool (DSS-5402) or NC Trauma Screening<br>Tool(DSS-6195A/B) for additional considerations andquestions.|that could severely compromise the safety of a child or youth who<br>is missing to include a parent's refusal to report their child missing.<br>When the local county child welfare agency has the responsibility<br>for care and placement of a child, there is the requirement to<br>make notification to the National Center for Missing and Exploited<br>Children (NCMEC) in addition to law enforcement. For foster care<br>cases with an open Assessment, the assigned Permanency<br>caseworker is best suited to provide this notification because they<br>will have the most information about the child's appearance, last<br>known location, and whereabouts. Please see Permanency<br>Planningfor requirements to report.<br>Contacts with Missing Children and Youth<br>If an In-Home caseworker is able to make contact with the missing<br>child/youth, helpful information to attempt to gather from the<br>child or youth would be:<br>•<br>The child's location at the time of contact;<br>•<br>Any information about where they are staying currently or for<br>any period;<br>•<br>Any information about the individuals they may be with;<br>•<br>Information about the health and safety of the child/youth (if<br>parenting, also inquire about the health and safety of the<br>infant or child);<br>•<br>Whether they are attending school and where;<br>•<br>Whether they are employed and where; and<br>•<br>Any contact they have made with family members, friends,<br>probation and parole agents, etc.<br>If possible, consider referencing the Child Welfare Human<br>Trafficking Screening Tool (DSS-5402) or NC Trauma Screening<br>Tool(DSS-6195A/B) for additional considerations andquestions.|that could severely compromise the safety of a child or youth who<br>is missing to include a parent's refusal to report their child missing.<br>When the local county child welfare agency has the responsibility<br>for care and placement of a child, there is the requirement to<br>make notification to the National Center for Missing and Exploited<br>Children (NCMEC) in addition to law enforcement. For foster care<br>cases with an open Assessment, the assigned Permanency<br>caseworker is best suited to provide this notification because they<br>will have the most information about the child's appearance, last<br>known location, and whereabouts. Please see Permanency<br>Planningfor requirements to report.<br>Contacts with Missing Children and Youth<br>If an In-Home caseworker is able to make contact with the missing<br>child/youth, helpful information to attempt to gather from the<br>child or youth would be:<br>•<br>The child's location at the time of contact;<br>•<br>Any information about where they are staying currently or for<br>any period;<br>•<br>Any information about the individuals they may be with;<br>•<br>Information about the health and safety of the child/youth (if<br>parenting, also inquire about the health and safety of the<br>infant or child);<br>•<br>Whether they are attending school and where;<br>•<br>Whether they are employed and where; and<br>•<br>Any contact they have made with family members, friends,<br>probation and parole agents, etc.<br>If possible, consider referencing the Child Welfare Human<br>Trafficking Screening Tool (DSS-5402) or NC Trauma Screening<br>Tool(DSS-6195A/B) for additional considerations andquestions.|

# Required Contacts for In Home Services

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|DOCUMENTING CASE CONTACTS<br>The In-Home caseworker is required to assess for safety and progress at each<br>contact. All information obtained must be documented in 'Case Contacts' within<br>PATH NC. Each contact must include discussions regarding safety, risk, and well-<br>being. All requested information in the Case Contact must be gathered each<br>month and can be done over the course of multiple visits. Case contacts must be<br>reviewed by an agency supervisor.<br>When the contact occurs in the home, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document at least monthly the physical home environment to include:<br>•<br>The condition of the home for any safety hazards;<br>•<br>Identification of whether a tour of the home was completed, including<br>any buildings outside of the home that the child is accessing;<br>•<br>Firearm storage;<br>•<br>Functioning smoke alarms; and<br>•<br>Sleeping arrangements, including a separate space free of any of other<br>items for infants under 12 months.<br>Regardless of location of the contact, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document:<br>•<br>Participants in the interaction, discussions, child's statement and/or<br>observations<br>•<br>Safety Plan, if active (effectiveness, changes needed)<br>•<br>Changes in the household (new childcare/pets/household members,<br>remodeling, new job or financial status, etc.)<br>•<br>Whether individuals have privacy, an ability to set personal boundaries,<br>and adequate supervision to maintain safety<br>•<br>When applicable, progress to eliminate the need for a TSP<br>•<br>Observations of the family's interactions|DOCUMENTING CASE CONTACTS<br>The In-Home caseworker is required to assess for safety and progress at each<br>contact. All information obtained must be documented in 'Case Contacts' within<br>PATH NC. Each contact must include discussions regarding safety, risk, and well-<br>being. All requested information in the Case Contact must be gathered each<br>month and can be done over the course of multiple visits. Case contacts must be<br>reviewed by an agency supervisor.<br>When the contact occurs in the home, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document at least monthly the physical home environment to include:<br>•<br>The condition of the home for any safety hazards;<br>•<br>Identification of whether a tour of the home was completed, including<br>any buildings outside of the home that the child is accessing;<br>•<br>Firearm storage;<br>•<br>Functioning smoke alarms; and<br>•<br>Sleeping arrangements, including a separate space free of any of other<br>items for infants under 12 months.<br>Regardless of location of the contact, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document:<br>•<br>Participants in the interaction, discussions, child's statement and/or<br>observations<br>•<br>Safety Plan, if active (effectiveness, changes needed)<br>•<br>Changes in the household (new childcare/pets/household members,<br>remodeling, new job or financial status, etc.)<br>•<br>Whether individuals have privacy, an ability to set personal boundaries,<br>and adequate supervision to maintain safety<br>•<br>When applicable, progress to eliminate the need for a TSP<br>•<br>Observations of the family's interactions|DOCUMENTING CASE CONTACTS<br>The In-Home caseworker is required to assess for safety and progress at each<br>contact. All information obtained must be documented in 'Case Contacts' within<br>PATH NC. Each contact must include discussions regarding safety, risk, and well-<br>being. All requested information in the Case Contact must be gathered each<br>month and can be done over the course of multiple visits. Case contacts must be<br>reviewed by an agency supervisor.<br>When the contact occurs in the home, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document at least monthly the physical home environment to include:<br>•<br>The condition of the home for any safety hazards;<br>•<br>Identification of whether a tour of the home was completed, including<br>any buildings outside of the home that the child is accessing;<br>•<br>Firearm storage;<br>•<br>Functioning smoke alarms; and<br>•<br>Sleeping arrangements, including a separate space free of any of other<br>items for infants under 12 months.<br>Regardless of location of the contact, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document:<br>•<br>Participants in the interaction, discussions, child's statement and/or<br>observations<br>•<br>Safety Plan, if active (effectiveness, changes needed)<br>•<br>Changes in the household (new childcare/pets/household members,<br>remodeling, new job or financial status, etc.)<br>•<br>Whether individuals have privacy, an ability to set personal boundaries,<br>and adequate supervision to maintain safety<br>•<br>When applicable, progress to eliminate the need for a TSP<br>•<br>Observations of the family's interactions|In PATH NC when a 'case contact' is selected to document visits in<br>the home, a series of questions will be included for the In-Home<br>caseworker to document. This is designed to ensure that In-Home<br>caseworkers complete quality contacts with families that ensure<br>safety. The required questions will be formulated based on the<br>location selected for the contact.<br>The case contact guides the information that is documented to<br>include:<br>•<br>Focused discussion and attention on safety, risk, and well-<br>being of children and family;<br>•<br>Timely documentation of the home visit;<br>•<br>Follow-up on identified needs; and<br>•<br>Supports movement toward the intended objectives on<br>the IHFCP<br>For new household members over the age of 16, background<br>checks are required.||||

Case contacts must be<br>reviewed by an agency supervisor.<br>When the contact occurs in the home, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document at least monthly the physical home environment to include:<br>•<br>The condition of the home for any safety hazards;<br>•<br>Identification of whether a tour of the home was completed, including<br>any buildings outside of the home that the child is accessing;<br>•<br>Firearm storage;<br>•<br>Functioning smoke alarms; and<br>•<br>Sleeping arrangements, including a separate space free of any of other<br>items for infants under 12 months.<br>Regardless of location of the contact, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document:<br>•<br>Participants in the interaction, discussions, child's statement and/or<br>observations<br>•<br>Safety Plan, if active (effectiveness, changes needed)<br>•<br>Changes in the household (new childcare/pets/household members,<br>remodeling, new job or financial status, etc.)<br>•<br>Whether individuals have privacy, an ability to set personal boundaries,<br>and adequate supervision to maintain safety<br>•<br>When applicable, progress to eliminate the need for a TSP<br>•<br>Observations of the family's interactions|In PATH NC when a 'case contact' is selected to document visits in<br>the home, a series of questions will be included for the In-Home<br>caseworker to document. This is designed to ensure that In-Home<br>caseworkers complete quality contacts with families that ensure<br>safety. The required questions will be formulated based on the<br>location selected for the contact.<br>The case contact guides the information that is documented to<br>include:<br>•<br>Focused discussion and attention on safety, risk, and well-<br>being of children and family;<br>•<br>Timely documentation of the home visit;<br>•<br>Follow-up on identified needs; and<br>•<br>Supports movement toward the intended objectives on<br>the IHFCP<br>For new household members over the age of 16, background<br>checks are required.|caseworkers complete quality contacts with families that ensure|caseworkers complete quality contacts with families that ensure|caseworkers complete quality contacts with families that ensure|

All information obtained must be documented in 'Case Contacts' within<br>PATH NC. Each contact must include discussions regarding safety, risk, and well-<br>being. All requested information in the Case Contact must be gathered each<br>month and can be done over the course of multiple visits. Case contacts must be<br>reviewed by an agency supervisor.<br>When the contact occurs in the home, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document at least monthly the physical home environment to include:<br>•<br>The condition of the home for any safety hazards;<br>•<br>Identification of whether a tour of the home was completed, including<br>any buildings outside of the home that the child is accessing;<br>•<br>Firearm storage;<br>•<br>Functioning smoke alarms; and<br>•<br>Sleeping arrangements, including a separate space free of any of other<br>items for infants under 12 months.<br>Regardless of location of the contact, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document:<br>•<br>Participants in the interaction, discussions, child's statement and/or<br>observations<br>•<br>Safety Plan, if active (effectiveness, changes needed)<br>•<br>Changes in the household (new childcare/pets/household members,<br>remodeling, new job or financial status, etc.)<br>•<br>Whether individuals have privacy, an ability to set personal boundaries,<br>and adequate supervision to maintain safety<br>•<br>When applicable, progress to eliminate the need for a TSP<br>•<br>Observations of the family's interactions|DOCUMENTING CASE CONTACTS<br>The In-Home caseworker is required to assess for safety and progress at each<br>contact. Case contacts must be<br>reviewed by an agency supervisor.<br>When the contact occurs in the home, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document at least monthly the physical home environment to include:<br>•<br>The condition of the home for any safety hazards;<br>•<br>Identification of whether a tour of the home was completed, including<br>any buildings outside of the home that the child is accessing;<br>•<br>Firearm storage;<br>•<br>Functioning smoke alarms; and<br>•<br>Sleeping arrangements, including a separate space free of any of other<br>items for infants under 12 months.<br>Regardless of location of the contact, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document:<br>•<br>Participants in the interaction, discussions, child's statement and/or<br>observations<br>•<br>Safety Plan, if active (effectiveness, changes needed)<br>•<br>Changes in the household (new childcare/pets/household members,<br>remodeling, new job or financial status, etc.)<br>•<br>Whether individuals have privacy, an ability to set personal boundaries,<br>and adequate supervision to maintain safety<br>•<br>When applicable, progress to eliminate the need for a TSP<br>•<br>Observations of the family's interactions|DOCUMENTING CASE CONTACTS<br>The In-Home caseworker is required to assess for safety and progress at each<br>contact. All information obtained must be documented in 'Case Contacts' within<br>PATH NC. Each contact must include discussions regarding safety, risk, and well-<br>being. All requested information in the Case Contact must be gathered each<br>month and can be done over the course of multiple visits. Case contacts must be<br>reviewed by an agency supervisor.<br>When the contact occurs in the home, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document at least monthly the physical home environment to include:<br>•<br>The condition of the home for any safety hazards;<br>•<br>Identification of whether a tour of the home was completed, including<br>any buildings outside of the home that the child is accessing;<br>•<br>Firearm storage;<br>•<br>Functioning smoke alarms; and<br>•<br>Sleeping arrangements, including a separate space free of any of other<br>items for infants under 12 months.<br>Regardless of location of the contact, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document:<br>•<br>Participants in the interaction, discussions, child's statement and/or<br>observations<br>•<br>Safety Plan, if active (effectiveness, changes needed)<br>•<br>Changes in the household (new childcare/pets/household members,<br>remodeling, new job or financial status, etc.)<br>•<br>Whether individuals have privacy, an ability to set personal boundaries,<br>and adequate supervision to maintain safety<br>•<br>When applicable, progress to eliminate the need for a TSP<br>•<br>Observations of the family's interactions|In PATH NC when a 'case contact' is selected to document visits in<br>the home, a series of questions will be included for the In-Home<br>caseworker to document. This is designed to ensure that In-Home<br>caseworkers complete quality contacts with families that ensure<br>safety. The required questions will be formulated based on the<br>location selected for the contact.<br>The case contact guides the information that is documented to<br>include:<br>•<br>Focused discussion and attention on safety, risk, and well-<br>being of children and family;<br>•<br>Timely documentation of the home visit;<br>•<br>Follow-up on identified needs; and<br>•<br>Supports movement toward the intended objectives on<br>the IHFCP<br>For new household members over the age of 16, background<br>checks are required.|include:|the IHFCP|the IHFCP|

This is designed to ensure that In-Home<br>caseworkers complete quality contacts with families that ensure<br>safety. The required questions will be formulated based on the<br>location selected for the contact.<br>The case contact guides the information that is documented to<br>include:<br>•<br>Focused discussion and attention on safety, risk, and well-<br>being of children and family;<br>•<br>Timely documentation of the home visit;<br>•<br>Follow-up on identified needs; and<br>•<br>Supports movement toward the intended objectives on<br>the IHFCP<br>For new household members over the age of 16, background<br>checks are required.||||

Case contacts must be<br>reviewed by an agency supervisor.<br>When the contact occurs in the home, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document at least monthly the physical home environment to include:<br>•<br>The condition of the home for any safety hazards;<br>•<br>Identification of whether a tour of the home was completed, including<br>any buildings outside of the home that the child is accessing;<br>•<br>Firearm storage;<br>•<br>Functioning smoke alarms; and<br>•<br>Sleeping arrangements, including a separate space free of any of other<br>items for infants under 12 months.<br>Regardless of location of the contact, the In-Home caseworker must review and<br>document:<br>•<br>Participants in the interaction, discussions, child's statement and/or<br>observations<br>•<br>Safety Plan, if active (effectiveness, changes needed)<br>•<br>Changes in the household (new childcare/pets/household members,<br>remodeling, new job or financial status, etc.)<br>•<br>Whether individuals have privacy, an ability to set personal boundaries,<br>and adequate supervision to maintain safety<br>•<br>When applicable, progress to eliminate the need for a TSP<br>•<br>Observations of the family's interactions|DOCUMENTING CASE CONTACTS<br>The In-Home caseworker is required to assess for safety and progress at each<br>contact. # In Home Family Case Plan

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|When an allegation is Substantiated or found to be Child Protective Services<br>Needed, and the family is transferred for ongoing services, the local county<br>child welfare agency must:<br>•<br>Coordinate, provide for,maintain,and arrange interventions and<br>services that focus on child safety and protection, family<br>preservation and the prevention of further abuse or neglect; and<br>•<br>Document diligent efforts to engage and gain participation from the<br>family.<br>The In-Home caseworker must develop the initial IHFCP and any formal<br>reviews of IHFCP during a Child/Family Team Meeting(CFT) if there is an active<br>Safety Plan (Part E of the Safety Assessment) in place to ensure immediate<br>safety of the child(ren). The CFT must include all Safety Network members<br>identified in the Safety Plan.<br>The In-Home Family Case Plan (IHFCP) should be completed in PATH NC. Refer<br>to the Instructions( DSS-5239ins PATH NC) for more information. The IHFCP<br>must be developed with the family tocreate a basis for providing services with<br>the primary goal of maintaining the child(ren) safely in the home of the<br>parent/caretaker. It must:<br>•<br>Document whether each child(on an individual basis) isat serious<br>and/or imminent risk ofenteringfoster careabsent prevention<br>services. For children so identified, theIHFCPmust document why<br>they are at riskin behaviorally specific languageand which services are<br>directed at preventing removal from the home.<br>•<br>Be based on the information obtained from theSafety Assessment,<br>Risk Assessment, Family Strengths and Needs Assessment,Child<br>Strengths and Needs Assessment (when applicable), Continuing Needs<br>and Safety Requirements,Trauma Screening Tools, and other<br>assessments regarding the needs of the child(ren) and family;|When an allegation is Substantiated or found to be Child Protective Services<br>Needed, and the family is transferred for ongoing services, the local county<br>child welfare agency must:<br>•<br>Coordinate, provide for,maintain,and arrange interventions and<br>services that focus on child safety and protection, family<br>preservation and the prevention of further abuse or neglect; and<br>•<br>Document diligent efforts to engage and gain participation from the<br>family.<br>The In-Home caseworker must develop the initial IHFCP and any formal<br>reviews of IHFCP during a Child/Family Team Meeting(CFT) if there is an active<br>Safety Plan (Part E of the Safety Assessment) in place to ensure immediate<br>safety of the child(ren). The IHFCP<br>must be developed with the family tocreate a basis for providing services with<br>the primary goal of maintaining the child(ren) safely in the home of the<br>parent/caretaker. It must:<br>•<br>Document whether each child(on an individual basis) isat serious<br>and/or imminent risk ofenteringfoster careabsent prevention<br>services. For children so identified, theIHFCPmust document why<br>they are at riskin behaviorally specific languageand which services are<br>directed at preventing removal from the home.<br>•<br>Be based on the information obtained from theSafety Assessment,<br>Risk Assessment, Family Strengths and Needs Assessment,Child<br>Strengths and Needs Assessment (when applicable), Continuing Needs<br>and Safety Requirements,Trauma Screening Tools, and other<br>assessments regarding the needs of the child(ren) and family;|When an allegation is Substantiated or found to be Child Protective Services<br>Needed, and the family is transferred for ongoing services, the local county<br>child welfare agency must:<br>•<br>Coordinate, provide for,maintain,and arrange interventions and<br>services that focus on child safety and protection, family<br>preservation and the prevention of further abuse or neglect; and<br>•<br>Document diligent efforts to engage and gain participation from the<br>family.<br>The In-Home caseworker must develop the initial IHFCP and any formal<br>reviews of IHFCP during a Child/Family Team Meeting(CFT) if there is an active<br>Safety Plan (Part E of the Safety Assessment) in place to ensure immediate<br>safety of the child(ren). The CFT must include all Safety Network members<br>identified in the Safety Plan.<br>The In-Home Family Case Plan (IHFCP) should be completed in PATH NC. Refer<br>to the Instructions( DSS-5239ins PATH NC) for more information. The IHFCP<br>must be developed with the family tocreate a basis for providing services with<br>the primary goal of maintaining the child(ren) safely in the home of the<br>parent/caretaker. It must:<br>•<br>Document whether each child(on an individual basis) isat serious<br>and/or imminent risk ofenteringfoster careabsent prevention<br>services. For children so identified, theIHFCPmust document why<br>they are at riskin behaviorally specific languageand which services are<br>directed at preventing removal from the home.<br>•<br>Be based on the information obtained from theSafety Assessment,<br>Risk Assessment, Family Strengths and Needs Assessment,Child<br>Strengths and Needs Assessment (when applicable), Continuing Needs<br>and Safety Requirements,Trauma Screening Tools, and other<br>assessments regarding the needs of the child(ren) and family;|The In-Homecaseworker shouldsupport the family and their Safety<br>Network in developing and demonstrating safety for the child(ren).<br>The In-Homecaseworkershould be anactive participant in the<br>identification and implementation of services/activities with the<br>family.<br>While it is required to develop the IHFCP in the context of a CFT<br>when there is an active safety plan in place, it can be beneficial to<br>develop all IHFCP using a CFT approach. Engaging a family's Safety<br>Networks and supports allows everyone invested in the safety of the<br>children to build a more comprehensive, long term plan for safety<br>that reduces the local county child welfare agency's need to<br>continue involuntary services with the family.Meaningful<br>engagement of allSafety Networkmembers can be facilitated by<br>following CFT protocol and guidance.<br>If the birth mother or father are not included in the plan, the name<br>of the caretaker responsible should be used.<br>The IHFCP developed with the family should be what the family and<br>Safety Network see as important as well as formal and informal<br>assessments of the family.Other assessments by the In-Home<br>caseworker or other professional providers should be used in the<br>development and/or review of theIHFCP. <br>InPATH NC, the caseworker will get a task to complete the IHFCP<br>within 30 days once the case has been opened to In-Home services.<br>The specification that absent effective services a child is at serious or<br>imminent risk of foster care is a federal requirement to receive Title|The In-Homecaseworker shouldsupport the family and their Safety<br>Network in developing and demonstrating safety for the child(ren).<br>The In-Homecaseworkershould be anactive participant in the<br>identification and implementation of services/activities with the<br>family.<br>While it is required to develop the IHFCP in the context of a CFT<br>when there is an active safety plan in place, it can be beneficial to<br>develop all IHFCP using a CFT approach. The In-Home caseworker is required to discuss<br>this with the family when it is determined that any child in the home<br>is considered to be at serious and/or imminent risk of entering<br>foster care. Please see the serious and/or imminent risk section for<br>more details.<br>As a part of the IHFCP, caseworkers are required to include that<br>removal is a potential outcome if safety cannot be ensured. Rule<br>10A NCAC 70A .0107 (d) includes a requirement that every case plan<br>documents that the plan is removal of the child(ren) from the home<br>if the family is not able to mitigate safety concerns. This is not the<br>same thing as a child being at serious or imminent risk of removal<br>and eligible to be a Candidate for Foster Care.<br>[NOTE: Every child whose family requires In-Home services does not<br>qualify to receive Title IV-E reimbursement for services. A family's<br>need to "to improve the quality of child care, to be more adequate<br>parents, guardians, or caretakers, and to preserve and stabilize<br>family life" as found in N.C.G.S. § 7B-300 allows families who need<br>involuntary intervention to ensure safety, but whose circumstances<br>do not meet the criteria for serious and/or imminent risk to receive<br>In-Home services.]<br>Families have to be made aware ofwhat actionsthe local county<br>child welfare agencywill take if the safetyconcernsand future risk<br>of harmcannot be mitigated. # SERIOUS AND/OR IMMINENT RISK CRITERIA

| | Protocol-What you must do | | | Guidance-How you should do it | |

|**SERIOUS AND/OR IMMINENT RISK OF ENTERING FOSTER CARE**<br>For cases entering CPS In-Home, an initial determination of serious<br>and/or imminent risk eligibility must be made based on information<br>gathered during the CPS-A and provided at case decision. A child may be<br>considered at serious and/or imminent risk of entering foster care in CPS<br>In-Home when:<br>•<br>The CPS-Assessment case decision was "Substantiated" or<br>"Services Needed"<br>•<br>The most recent Safety Assessment has a determination of "safe<br>with a plan," and the agency is still actively monitoring the safety<br>interventions in that plan<br>•<br>ANDthe agency would pursue custody if the danger indicators<br>cannot be controlled by interventions**.**<br>The determination of eligibility must be made on an individual basis for<br>each child receiving In-Home services.<br>If the child meets serious and/or imminent risk of entering foster care<br>criteria the In-Home caseworker must develop a plan **WITH** the family<br>that is documented on the In-Home Family Case Plan (IHFCP) that:<br>•<br>describes the reason the child is at imminent risk of entering<br>care, and<br>•<br>the service(s)/activities to be offered or provided to safely<br>prevent the need for removal.<br>•<br>Review and refer to Evidenced Based Prevention Service Policy if<br>a service from NC Title IV-E Evidence-Based Prevention Services<br>In the absence of a case plan, evidence of court proceedings for removal<br>also serves as documentation for serious and/or imminent risk. <!-- page: 42 -->
# SERIOUS AND/OR IMMINENT RISK CRITERIA

|The In-Home caseworker must communicate with the family that the<br>child is at serious or imminent risk of entering foster care without<br>effective services and clearly outline what changes are required to<br>prevent removal.<br>IN-HOME FAMILY CASE PLAN COMPLETION FOR SERIOUS AND/OR<br>IMMINENT RISK CASES<br>An IHFCP must be developed for every child receiving In-Home services.<br>All children who are determined to be at serious and/or imminent risk<br>eligible must have a case plan developed WITH the family. Services on<br>the case plan must address the identified safety concerns that place the<br>child at serious and/or imminent risk of removal.<br>The meeting to develop the IHFCP with children who meet serious and/or<br>imminent risk criteria must include:<br>• Communication with the family that the child(ren) is at serious<br>and/or imminent risk of entering foster care without preventative<br>measures and behaviorally specific language that explains the<br>reason for that risk;<br>• Review of any services/activities already agreed upon by the<br>family and status of the commencement of those<br>services/activities;<br>• Information about any additional available services needed to<br>prevent foster care; and<br>• Agreement between the family and the agency regarding which<br>services/activities will be used to reduce the risk of removal.|explained in a non-threatening way that clearly outlines possible<br>outcomes. If it would be untrue to tell a family that the plan is<br>foster care if they are not able or willing to engage in the<br>services/activities to achieve the behavioral change required, the<br>child likely does not meet the criteria for serious and/or imminent<br>risk.<br>IN-HOME FAMILY CASE PLAN COMPLETION FOR SERIOUS<br>AND/OR IMMINENT RISK CASES<br>Once eligibility has been determined for serious and/or<br>imminent risk, the development of the case plan for children<br>who are eligible does not differ from the process to complete<br>case plans during the provision of In-Home services.|
|---|---|

In Home Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (May 2026) Page 42 NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL Return to In Home Services TOC Return to Manual TOC

<!-- page: 43 -->
# SERIOUS AND/OR IMMINENT RISK CRITERIA

|IV-E FUNDING DURING IN-HOME SERVICES<br>All children who are at serious and/or imminent risk of entering foster<br>care and have a plan developed with the family are eligible to receive IV-E<br>funds.<br>The In-Home caseworker must document behaviorally specific serious<br>and/or imminent risk eligibility AND develop a case plan with the family<br>to claim IV-E funds (code for Z) for case management services. In cases<br>where the child does not or no longer meets the eligibility for serious<br>and/or imminent risk, another form of funding must be used. See the CPS<br>Funding Manual for additional information on the most accurate funding<br>sources.<br>The In-Home caseworker must only code allowable services for IV-E<br>funding. Examples of activities that can be billed include, but are not<br>limited to:<br>• Safety planning with the family<br>• On-going visits and collateral contacts to assess safety and review<br>compliance with safety plans<br>• The assessment of possible Temporary Safety Providers and<br>placement providers<br>• Assessing the service needs of a family and connecting them with<br>services<br>• Searching for and engaging relatives<br>• Preparing a family to transition to CPS In-Home services|IV-E FUNDING DURING IN-HOME SERVICES<br>Federal policy allows counties to claim reimbursement for<br>specific allowable costs when serving children who are at<br>serious and/or imminent risk of entering foster care absent<br>preventative services and a case plan exists that was developed<br>WITH the family. Title IV-E reimbursement (coding for Z) is<br>limited to children who would qualify for Title IV-E foster care<br>maintenance payments consistent with section 472(i)(2) of the<br>Social Security Act. A local county child welfare agency can be<br>reimbursed for their work with families, based on Section<br>471(a)(15)(B)(i) of the Social Security Act, when they are either<br>pursuing removal of the child(ren) from the home OR making<br>reasonable efforts to prevent such removal.<br>For In-Home services, the local county child welfare agency's<br>involvement with the child(ren) and their family must be to<br>specifically address the needs to prevent removal. This allows<br>counties to claim IV-E funds for case management services<br>provided to a child when they meet the criteria for serious<br>and/or imminent risk. The local county child welfare agency can<br>no longer claim reimbursement (code for Z) when the child(ren)<br>no longer meets the criteria for being at serious and/or<br>imminent risk or when there is no current case plan. North<br>Carolina requires the IHFCP to be reviewed with the family<br>every 90 days. See Appendix 3.4 Child Welfare Funding-CPS for<br>additional information on costs that can and cannot be claimed<br>to IV-E and on other sources of funding.|
|---|---|

# Review of Services

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|DOMESTIC VIOLENCE<br>A separateIHFCP must be completed with the non-offending<br>parent/adult victim and the perpetrator of domestic violence.<br>The perpetrator of domestic violence must not have access to the non-<br>offending parent/adult victim'sIHFCP. <br>In cases where there are concerns for domestic violence, the IHFCP must<br>focus on:<br>`o` Reducing the risk of child maltreatment that are the result of<br>unsafe interactions between the caretakers; and<br>`o` Strengthening parenting ability to ensure that both the<br>perpetrator of domestic violence and the non-offending<br>parent/adult victim are making choices that ensure safety for the<br>child(ren).<br>Services/Activitieson the IHFCP must be responsive to:<br>`o` Foster perpetrators of domestic violence taking responsibility to<br>stop their acts of violence and their own behavioral change; and<br>`o` Enhance the non-offending parent's/adult victim's capacity and<br>willingness to protect the children.<br>The capacity and willingness of the non-offending parent/adult victim to<br>take appropriate actions to protect the child are issues that must be<br>assessed and monitored during the provision of CPS In-Home Services.<br>The non-offending parent/adult victim must not be held responsible for<br>the domestic violence perpetrator's failure to follow through with<br>services.|DOMESTIC VIOLENCE<br>A separateIHFCP must be completed with the non-offending<br>parent/adult victim and the perpetrator of domestic violence.<br>The perpetrator of domestic violence must not have access to the non-<br>offending parent/adult victim'sIHFCP. <br>In cases where there are concerns for domestic violence, the IHFCP must<br>focus on:<br>`o` Reducing the risk of child maltreatment that are the result of<br>unsafe interactions between the caretakers; and<br>`o` Strengthening parenting ability to ensure that both the<br>perpetrator of domestic violence and the non-offending<br>parent/adult victim are making choices that ensure safety for the<br>child(ren).<br>Services/Activitieson the IHFCP must be responsive to:<br>`o` Foster perpetrators of domestic violence taking responsibility to<br>stop their acts of violence and their own behavioral change; and<br>`o` Enhance the non-offending parent's/adult victim's capacity and<br>willingness to protect the children.<br>The capacity and willingness of the non-offending parent/adult victim to<br>take appropriate actions to protect the child are issues that must be<br>assessed and monitored during the provision of CPS In-Home Services.<br>The non-offending parent/adult victim must not be held responsible for<br>the domestic violence perpetrator's failure to follow through with<br>services.|Domestic Violence<br>The domestic violence scaled assessment tools: Non-Offending<br>Parent/Adult Victim DV Assessment Tool, DSS-5235, DV Perpetrator<br>Assessment Tool DSS-5234) are designed to support the In-Home<br>caseworker during the ongoing assessment of whether safety and risk<br>factors have been addressed. The Personalized DV Safety Plan, DSS-5233,<br>and Children's Domestic Violence Assessment Tool DSS-5237 are required<br>to be considered for use and updated with the domestic violence victim<br>parent/caretaker.<br>There is not a specific timeframe for when the home environment is<br>considered safe orthe risk reduced to the point where the family no<br>longer needs involuntary services. Factors to consider in assessing change<br>in behavior include:<br>•<br>Family interaction;<br>•<br>Criminal behavior; and<br>•<br>Environment of the home.|Domestic Violence<br>The domestic violence scaled assessment tools: Non-Offending<br>Parent/Adult Victim DV Assessment Tool, DSS-5235, DV Perpetrator<br>Assessment Tool DSS-5234) are designed to support the In-Home<br>caseworker during the ongoing assessment of whether safety and risk<br>factors have been addressed. The Personalized DV Safety Plan, DSS-5233,<br>and Children's Domestic Violence Assessment Tool DSS-5237 are required<br>to be considered for use and updated with the domestic violence victim<br>parent/caretaker.<br>There is not a specific timeframe for when the home environment is<br>considered safe orthe risk reduced to the point where the family no<br>longer needs involuntary services. Factors to consider in assessing change<br>in behavior include:<br>•<br>Family interaction;<br>•<br>Criminal behavior; and<br>•<br>Environment of the home.|Domestic Violence<br>The domestic violence scaled assessment tools: Non-Offending<br>Parent/Adult Victim DV Assessment Tool, DSS-5235, DV Perpetrator<br>Assessment Tool DSS-5234) are designed to support the In-Home<br>caseworker during the ongoing assessment of whether safety and risk<br>factors have been addressed. The Personalized DV Safety Plan, DSS-5233,<br>and Children's Domestic Violence Assessment Tool DSS-5237 are required<br>to be considered for use and updated with the domestic violence victim<br>parent/caretaker.<br>There is not a specific timeframe for when the home environment is<br>considered safe orthe risk reduced to the point where the family no<br>longer needs involuntary services. Factors to consider in assessing change<br>in behavior include:<br>•<br>Family interaction;<br>•<br>Criminal behavior; and<br>•<br>Environment of the home.|

# Review of Services

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|CHILD WELL-BEING<br>Every In-Home Services case must identify and document child well-being<br>within 30 daysof the case decision. All children who have needs<br>identified on the Child Strength and Needs Assessment that are not<br>impacting safety and being addressed in the caretaker's objectives must<br>be included in the IHFCP and documentation must reflect the In-Home<br>caseworker's efforts to support the family in addressing them.<br>If not applicable,documentation must reflect that there are no identified<br>well-being concerns for the child(ren).|CHILD WELL-BEING<br>Every In-Home Services case must identify and document child well-being<br>within 30 daysof the case decision. All children who have needs<br>identified on the Child Strength and Needs Assessment that are not<br>impacting safety and being addressed in the caretaker's objectives must<br>be included in the IHFCP and documentation must reflect the In-Home<br>caseworker's efforts to support the family in addressing them.<br>If not applicable,documentation must reflect that there are no identified<br>well-being concerns for the child(ren).|CHILD WELL-BEING<br>Every In-Home Services case must identify and document child well-being<br>within 30 daysof the case decision. The In-Home caseworker should assess all of the child's needs<br>and document each one. It is important for the In-Home caseworker to<br>consider how to address children's wellbeing needs and support<br>caretakers in developing a plan to address them.|The IHFCP includes a section to capture each child's wellbeing needs<br>separately. The In-Home caseworker should assess all of the child's needs<br>and document each one. |HUMAN TRAFFICKING<br>A child who is sold, traded, or exchanged for anything, or to settle a debt,<br>regardless of whether the child is used for labor or sex. Child welfare must<br>respond to address safety of the child(ren) regardless of the relationship<br>between the victim and the perpetrator. Local county child welfare agencies<br>must continue to assess the safety and well-being of children during the<br>provision of In-Home Services, including children suspected or confirmed to be<br>victims of human trafficking.<br>Local county child welfare agencies must identify and document appropriate<br>services for children who are believed to be or at risk of being victims of<br>human trafficking.<br>If, during the provision of In-Home services the caseworker becomes aware of<br>Human Trafficking, a new Intake report must be generated. The In-Home<br>caseworker must assess for the safety of the child with the caretaker.<br>Caseworkers must follow North Carolina child welfare policy and practice on<br>Human Trafficking. |A CFT must be used to develop and review the IHFCP when there is an active<br>Safety Plan in place and include the Safety Network member(s) involved in<br>ensuring the safety of the children. A CFT must be used in cases involving:<br>•<br>Children in the home of a TSP.<br>•<br>Children at serious and/or imminent risk of removal.<br>•<br>A juvenile petition without non-secure custody.<br>Local county child welfare agencies must use a CFT during In-Home Services:<br>~~•~~<br>To review theSafety Plan, if needed<br>•<br>For quarterly reviews of theIHFCP<br>•<br>To update theIHFCPto address safety or high-risk concerns, including,<br>but not limited to:<br>`o` Identification of a newdanger indicator; <br>`o` Next steps for lack of progress cases;<br>•<br>When requested by the family;<br>•<br>At critical decision points, to include possible out-of-home placement;<br>•<br>When a child is placed with a TSP and the parent cannot be located<br>and/or there is no parent to make decisions regarding the child;<br>•<br>At six months after development of theIHFCP and: <br>`o` There is a lack of progress as indicated by no activities completed<br>and any behavioral changes demonstrated that mitigate risk; or<br>`o` The child(ren) in the care of a TSPhave not been unable to return<br>home because immediate safety still exists; <br>•<br>Prior to and within 30 days of case closure in cases that are repeat<br>recipients of CPS In-Home or received Permanency Planning services to<br>specifically address the plan the family will follow to prevent repeat<br>maltreatment.|A CFT must be used to develop and review the IHFCP when there is an active|A CFT must be used to develop and review the IHFCP when there is an active|A CFT must be used to develop and review the IHFCP when there is an active|Three column mapping should be used to garner from the team<br>what is working, worries, and solutioning the best next steps. It is<br>important to ensure the family's voice is captured.Use of a neutral<br>facilitator is best practice for all CFT meetings. While a facilitator is<br>not required inall In-Homecases, it remains best practice as there<br>are many benefits to a facilitated meeting. A neutral facilitator can:<br>•<br>Keep the team focused on the identified purpose;<br>•<br>Ensure everyone is heard and engaged; and<br>•<br>Help elevate important points for plan development.<br>All Safety Network members who are a part of the Safety Plan are<br>required to be included in the CFT. However, the invitation should<br>be extended to as many additional supports as the family would like<br>to allow them provide additional perspectives. It also allows the<br>Safety and Support Networks to offer concrete ways support can be<br>provided which can be added to the plan.<br>One of the underlying beliefs of the family-centered approach<br>continues to be that the safety of the child is the first concern. The<br>caseworker should explain that the primary goal is to maintain the<br>child safely in the home of the caretaker, but if the child's safety is<br>compromised, the agency will take steps to ensure the safety of the<br>child. This may include filing a juvenile petitionand requesting non-<br>securecustody.<br>If an immediatedanger indicatoris identified, the agency must<br>respond. See Safety Planning protocol in Cross Function Topics in the<br>NC Child Welfare manual.|||

|LACK OF PROGRESS<br>Thelocal county child welfare agency must consider filing a juvenile<br>petition when there continues to be safety issues, risks of harm that place<br>the child(ren) atserious and/or imminent risk of removal and:<br>•<br>Efforts to engage a family are not successful; or<br>•<br>There is refusal to follow through with services; or<br>•<br>Participation is minimal and the family is receiving virtually no<br>benefits from the process; or<br>•<br>Sufficient and timely progress has not been made in addressing the<br>issues that led to the child abuse, neglect, and/or dependency; or<br>•<br>A case has been open for six months with a lack of progress and/or<br>the child(ren) have remained in a TSP.<br>This includes cases where the risk of maltreatmenthas not been reduced, and<br>the family is not making progress or simply not cooperating. |WHEN NO PROGRESS HAS BEEN MADE<br>Despite an agency's best efforts to engage the family and coordinate the<br>necessary remedial services during the provision of CPS In-Home Services,<br>families may not make sufficient and timely progress in addressing the issues<br>that led to the child abuse, neglect, and/or dependency. In these cases, the<br>agency in consultation with the county attorney must discuss the possibility of<br>filinga petition alleging that the child is abused, neglected, and/or dependent.<br>NCGS § 7B-302(c) requires the local county child welfare agency to provide<br>protective services and seek a court action if the parent/caretaker does not<br>cooperate.<br>Once abuse, neglect, and/or dependency have been adjudicated, the judge can<br>order the parents/caretaker to take certain steps toresolve the behaviors<br>or conditions that led to the filing of the petition.
